# CHAPTER 6
# 🔢 WEIGHTS, MEASURES, CONVERSION FORMULAE & CALCULATIONS

> ⚠️ **THIS IS THE HIGHEST-SCORING CHAPTER OF THE PAPER — AND THE ONE MOST OFTEN LOST.**
> Read it with a **pen and paper**. Work every example yourself before looking at the answer.

---

## 6.1 THE THREE SYSTEMS OF WEIGHTS & MEASURES

📌 Three systems have been used in pharmacy:

| System | Used for | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **1. METRIC (SI / decimal) system** | ⭐ **The only legally recognised system in India** (Standards of Weights & Measures Act) — used in IP, BP, USP | **Current** |
| **2. IMPERIAL / APOTHECARIES' system** | Old British pharmaceutical system — grains, drachms, ounces, minims | **Obsolete** (but examined!) |
| **3. AVOIRDUPOIS system** | Commercial weighing of goods — ounces & pounds | Commercial |

⭐ **Why the metric system is preferred:** it is **decimal** (all conversions are by powers of 10),
**simple, universal, less liable to error**, and legally required.

---

### 6.1.1 ⭐ THE METRIC SYSTEM

📌 **Fundamental units:** **metre (length), gram (mass), litre (volume)**.

🔢 **METRIC WEIGHT (MASS)**

| Unit | Symbol | Equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| **1 kilogram** | kg | **1000 g** |
| **1 hectogram** | hg | 100 g |
| **1 decagram** | dag | 10 g |
| **1 GRAM** | **g** | **1 g = the base unit** |
| **1 decigram** | dg | 0.1 g |
| **1 centigram** | cg | 0.01 g |
| **1 milligram** | mg | **0.001 g** → **1 g = 1000 mg** |
| **1 microgram** | µg (mcg) | **0.000001 g** → **1 mg = 1000 µg** |
| **1 nanogram** | ng | 0.001 µg |

🧠 **MEMORY LADDER:** **kg → g → mg → µg → ng**, each step **× 1000**.
🔢 **1 kg = 1000 g = 1 000 000 mg = 1 000 000 000 µg.**

🔢 **METRIC VOLUME (CAPACITY)**

| Unit | Symbol | Equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| **1 kilolitre** | kL | 1000 L |
| **1 LITRE** | **L** | **1000 mL** |
| **1 decilitre** | dL | 100 mL |
| **1 centilitre** | cL | 10 mL |
| **1 millilitre** | mL | **0.001 L** |
| **1 microlitre** | µL | 0.001 mL |

⭐ **1 mL = 1 cubic centimetre (cc/ cm³)** — for practical purposes identical.
⭐ **1 mL of WATER at 4 °C weighs exactly 1 g** → hence **1 L of water = 1 kg**.
⚠️ **TRAP** — this weight–volume identity holds **only for water**. For any other liquid,
`weight = volume × specific gravity`.

🔢 **METRIC LENGTH:** 1 m = 100 cm = 1000 mm; 1 cm = 10 mm; 1 mm = 1000 µm; 1 µm = 1000 nm;
**1 Å (Angstrom) = 10⁻¹⁰ m = 0.1 nm**.

---

### 6.1.2 ⭐ THE IMPERIAL / APOTHECARIES' SYSTEM

🔢 **APOTHECARIES' WEIGHT (Troy)** — *the "grain" system*

| Unit | Symbol | Equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| **20 grains (gr)** | ℈ | **1 scruple** |
| **3 scruples (60 grains)** | ℨ | **1 drachm (dram)** |
| **8 drachms (480 grains)** | ℥ | **1 ounce (apothecary/ troy)** |
| **12 ounces (5760 grains)** | ℔ | **1 pound (troy)** |

🧠 **MEMORY CHAIN — "20-3-8-12"**:
**20 grains = 1 scruple · 3 scruples = 1 drachm · 8 drachms = 1 ounce · 12 ounces = 1 pound.**

🔢 **IMPERIAL FLUID (LIQUID) MEASURE**

| Unit | Symbol | Equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| **60 minims (♏)** | fℨ | **1 fluid drachm** |
| **8 fluid drachms (480 minims)** | f℥ | **1 fluid ounce** |
| **20 fluid ounces** | — | **1 PINT** |
| **8 pints (160 fl oz)** | — | **1 GALLON** |
| **2 pints** | — | 1 quart |
| **4 quarts** | — | 1 gallon |

🧠 **MEMORY CHAIN — "60-8-20-8"**:
**60 minims = 1 fluid drachm · 8 fl drachms = 1 fluid ounce · 20 fl oz = 1 pint ·
8 pints = 1 gallon.**

⚠️ **TRAP — the IMPERIAL vs U.S. pint!**
* ⭐ **1 Imperial (British) pint = 20 fluid ounces = 568 mL** (often taken as **≈ 570 mL** or, in
  dispensing, **500 mL / 600 mL** as a practical figure).
* **1 U.S. pint = 16 fluid ounces = 473 mL.**
* **1 Imperial gallon = 4546 mL (≈ 4.5 L)**; **1 U.S. gallon = 3785 mL (≈ 3.8 L)**.

🔢 **AVOIRDUPOIS WEIGHT** (commercial)

| Unit | Equivalent |
|---|---|
| **1 ounce (av.)** | **437.5 grains = 28.35 g** |
| **16 ounces** | **1 pound (av.) = 7000 grains = 453.6 g** |
| **14 pounds** | 1 stone |
| **112 pounds** | 1 hundredweight (cwt) |
| **20 cwt** | 1 ton |

⚠️ **TRAP — the two "ounces" and two "pounds" are DIFFERENT!**

| | **Apothecary/ Troy** | **Avoirdupois** |
|---|---|---|
| **1 ounce** | **480 grains = 31.1 g** | **437.5 grains = 28.35 g** |
| **1 pound** | **5760 grains (12 oz) = 373.2 g** | **7000 grains (16 oz) = 453.6 g** |

🧠 **MEMORY HOOK** — "**The apothecary's ounce is HEAVIER (480 gr), but his pound is LIGHTER
(5760 gr).**" *(He has only 12 ounces to the pound, not 16.)*
🧠 **"A pound of feathers (av., 7000 gr) is heavier than a pound of gold (troy, 5760 gr) —
but an ounce of gold is heavier than an ounce of feathers."**

---

## 6.2 ⭐⭐ CONVERSION FORMULAE — "FROM ONE SCALE TO ANOTHER"

> 🔢 **THE MASTER CONVERSION TABLE — MEMORISE EVERY LINE.**

### A. WEIGHT conversions

| From | To | ⭐ Factor |
|---|---|---|
| **1 grain (gr)** | mg | ⭐ **64.8 mg** (practically **65 mg**) |
| **1 gram (g)** | grains | ⭐ **15.432 gr** (practically **15.4** or **15 gr**) |
| **1 scruple (20 gr)** | g | **1.296 g** (≈ 1.3 g) |
| **1 drachm, apoth. (60 gr)** | g | ⭐ **3.888 g** (practically **3.9 g** or **4 g**) |
| **1 ounce, apoth. (480 gr)** | g | ⭐ **31.1 g** (practically **31 g** or **30 g**) |
| **1 ounce, avoirdupois** | g | ⭐ **28.35 g** (practically **28 g** or **30 g**) |
| **1 pound, apoth./troy** | g | **373.2 g** |
| **1 pound, avoirdupois** | g | ⭐ **453.6 g** (practically **454 g** or **450 g**) |
| **1 kilogram** | pounds (av.) | ⭐ **2.2 lb** |
| **1 stone** | kg | 6.35 kg |

### B. VOLUME conversions

| From | To | ⭐ Factor |
|---|---|---|
| **1 minim (♏)** | mL | ⭐ **0.06 mL (0.0592 mL)** |
| **1 mL** | minims | ⭐ **16.9 minims (≈ 17)** |
| **1 fluid drachm (60 ♏)** | mL | ⭐ **3.55 mL (≈ 3.6 mL, practically 4 mL)** |
| **1 fluid ounce (Imperial)** | mL | ⭐ **28.4 mL (28.412)** — practically **28 mL** or **30 mL** |
| **1 fluid ounce (U.S.)** | mL | **29.57 mL (≈ 30 mL)** |
| **1 pint (Imperial)** | mL | ⭐ **568.25 mL (≈ 568 or 570 mL)** |
| **1 pint (U.S.)** | mL | **473 mL** |
| **1 gallon (Imperial)** | mL | ⭐ **4546 mL (4.546 L)** |
| **1 gallon (U.S.)** | mL | **3785 mL** |
| **1 litre** | fl oz (Imp.) | **35.2 fl oz** |
| **1 litre** | pints (Imp.) | **1.76 pints** |

### C. LENGTH & TEMPERATURE

| From | To | Factor |
|---|---|---|
| **1 inch** | cm | **2.54 cm** |
| **1 foot** | cm | 30.48 cm |
| **1 yard** | m | 0.914 m |
| **1 metre** | inches | 39.37 in |
| **1 mile** | km | 1.609 km |

🔢 **TEMPERATURE FORMULAE (asked as "formula for converting from one scale to the other")**
```
 °F = (9/5 × °C) + 32                    °C = 5/9 × (°F − 32)
 K  = °C + 273.15                        °R (Rankine) = °F + 459.67
```
🔢 **Reference points to memorise:**

| | °C | °F | K |
|---|---|---|---|
| Freezing point of water | **0** | **32** | 273 |
| **Normal body temperature** | ⭐ **37** | ⭐ **98.6** | 310 |
| Boiling point of water | **100** | **212** | 373 |
| Autoclave | **121** | 250 | 394 |
| Hot air oven | **160** | 320 | 433 |
| ⭐ **The scales READ THE SAME at** | ⭐ **−40** | ⭐ **−40** | — |

🔢 *Worked:* Convert 40 °C to °F → (9/5 × 40) + 32 = 72 + 32 = **104 °F.**
🔢 *Worked:* Convert 77 °F to °C → 5/9 × (77 − 32) = 5/9 × 45 = **25 °C.**

---

### 6.2.1 ⚠️ EXACT vs PRACTICAL (APPROXIMATE) EQUIVALENTS

📌 **A very frequently examined distinction.**

| Purpose | Which equivalents to use |
|---|---|
| ⭐ **Calculating a formula/ converting a prescription for compounding** (i.e. when the *quantity of drug* matters) | ⭐ **EXACT equivalents** (1 gr = 64.8 mg; 1 fl oz = 28.4 mL; 1 oz = 31.1 g) |
| ⭐ **Prescribing/ dispensing DOSES & directions to the patient**, and for **container sizes** | ⭐ **PRACTICAL (approximate) equivalents** (1 gr = 65 mg; 1 fl oz = 30 mL; 1 tsp = 5 mL) |

⭐ **THE COMMON "PRACTICAL" SET used in dispensing** 🔢

| Practical equivalence | |
|---|---|
| **1 grain ≈ 65 mg** | **1 g ≈ 15 grains** |
| **1 minim ≈ 0.06 mL** | **1 mL ≈ 17 minims** |
| **1 fluid drachm ≈ 4 mL** | **1 fluid ounce ≈ 30 mL** |
| **1 ounce ≈ 30 g** | **1 pint ≈ 500 mL (600 mL in older texts)** |
| **1 teaspoonful = 5 mL** | **1 tablespoonful = 15 mL** |
| **1 dessertspoonful = 8 mL** | **1 drop ≈ 0.05 mL (20 drops = 1 mL)** |

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE — converting an old prescription**
*Convert:* `℞ Sod. bicarb. gr. x; Aq. ad f℥ iv` *into metric.*
* gr x = 10 grains × 64.8 = **648 mg (0.648 g)** [exact] or 10 × 65 = **650 mg** [practical].
* f℥ iv = 4 fluid ounces × 28.4 = **113.6 mL** [exact] or 4 × 30 = **120 mL** [practical].
→ Metric prescription: **Sodium bicarbonate 650 mg, water to 120 mL.**

---

## 6.3 ⭐⭐ PERCENTAGE STRENGTHS & RATIO STRENGTH

### 6.3.1 THE FOUR KINDS OF PERCENTAGE — learn the definitions exactly

| Expression | 📌 Definition | Used for | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **% w/w** (weight in weight) | ⭐ **Number of GRAMS of solute in 100 GRAMS of the product** | ⭐ **Solid in solid, or semisolid** — ointments, creams, pastes, powders; also very viscous liquids | 2 % w/w salicylic acid ointment = 2 g in 100 g of ointment |
| **% w/v** (weight in volume) | ⭐ **Number of GRAMS of solute in 100 mL of the product** | ⭐ **Solid dissolved/ dispersed in a liquid** — the commonest in pharmacy: injections, lotions, mixtures, syrups | 0.9 % w/v NaCl = 0.9 g in 100 mL |
| **% v/v** (volume in volume) | ⭐ **Number of mL of solute in 100 mL of the product** | ⭐ **Liquid in liquid** — alcohol, volatile oils, glycerin solutions | 70 % v/v alcohol = 70 mL alcohol in 100 mL |
| **% v/w** (volume in weight) | ⭐ **Number of mL of solute in 100 GRAMS of the product** | ⭐ **Liquid in a solid/ semisolid** — rare; e.g. a volatile oil in an ointment or a powder | 2 % v/w coal tar in an ointment |

⚠️ **GOLDEN RULE:** *When a pharmacopoeial preparation says only **"per cent"** without a
qualifier:*
* ⭐ for **solutions of a solid in a liquid** → **% w/v**
* ⭐ for **solutions of a liquid in a liquid** → **% v/v**
* ⭐ for **mixtures of solids & semisolids** → **% w/w**
* ⭐ for **gases** → **% v/v**

🔢 **THE ONE FORMULA THAT SOLVES EVERYTHING**
```
              Quantity of active ingredient
Strength % = ───────────────────────────────── × 100
                Total quantity of product
```
And its rearrangement:
```
Quantity of ingredient  =  (Strength % × Total quantity) ÷ 100
```

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 1**
*How much potassium permanganate is needed to make 500 mL of a 0.05 % w/v solution?*
`= (0.05 × 500)/100 = 0.25 g = 250 mg` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 2**
*What is the percentage strength of a solution containing 6 g of boric acid in 300 mL?*
`= (6/300) × 100 = 2 % w/v` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 3 (w/w)**
*How much sulphur is present in 60 g of a 6 % w/w ointment?*
`= (6 × 60)/100 = 3.6 g of sulphur` (+ 56.4 g of base) ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 4 (v/v)**
*How much 90 % alcohol is required to make 250 mL of a 45 % v/v solution?*
`Alcohol needed = (45 × 250)/100 = 112.5 mL of absolute alcohol equivalent`
`Volume of 90 % alcohol = (112.5 × 100)/90 = 125 mL` → dilute to 250 mL with water ✔

### 6.3.2 ⭐ RATIO STRENGTH

📌 **Ratio strength** expresses a **very dilute** concentration as **1 part of the active ingredient
in a stated number of parts of the total product** — written as **1 : x** or "1 in x".

🔢 **CONVERSION FORMULAE**
```
                        100                                    100
Ratio strength 1 : x  →  ─── % ;        Percentage p %  →  1 : ───
                          x                                     p
```

🔢 **CONVERSION TABLE (memorise these!)**

| Ratio strength | Percentage | Ratio strength | Percentage |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1 : 10** | 10 % | **1 : 1000** | ⭐ **0.1 %** |
| **1 : 20** | 5 % | **1 : 2000** | 0.05 % |
| **1 : 50** | 2 % | **1 : 5000** | 0.02 % |
| **1 : 100** | 1 % | **1 : 10 000** | ⭐ **0.01 %** |
| **1 : 200** | 0.5 % | **1 : 100 000** | 0.001 % |
| **1 : 500** | 0.2 % | **1 : 1 000 000** | ⭐ **0.0001 % = 1 ppm** |

⭐ **Classic pharmaceutical ratio strengths:**
**Adrenaline injection 1 : 1000 (= 1 mg/mL)**; adrenaline for anaphylaxis **1 : 10 000** dilution;
**Potassium permanganate lotion 1 : 5000 to 1 : 10 000**; **Benzalkonium chloride 1 : 1000**;
**Lignocaine with adrenaline 1 : 80 000 (dental)**.

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 5**
*Express 1 : 2500 as a percentage.* → `100/2500 = 0.04 % w/v` ✔
🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 6**
*Express 0.25 % as a ratio strength.* → `100/0.25 = 400` → **1 : 400** ✔
🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 7**
*How much KMnO₄ is needed for 2 litres of a 1 : 5000 solution?*
1 : 5000 = 1 g in 5000 mL → for 2000 mL = `2000/5000 = 0.4 g = 400 mg` ✔

### 6.3.3 PARTS PER MILLION (ppm) & other expressions
🔢 **1 ppm = 1 part in 10⁶ = 0.0001 % = 1 mg per litre (for dilute aqueous solutions) = 1 µg/mL.**
* **mg/mL ↔ %:** ⭐ **1 % w/v = 10 mg/mL** (and **1 mg/mL = 0.1 %**).
* **mg % (mg per 100 mL)** — used for blood levels: 100 mg % = 1 mg/mL.
* **Molarity (M)** = moles of solute per **litre of solution**.
* **Molality (m)** = moles of solute per **kg of solvent**.
* **Normality (N)** = gram-equivalents per litre. `N = M × basicity/ acidity (valency)`.
* **Mole fraction** = moles of a component ÷ total moles.

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 8**
*A 5 % w/v dextrose infusion — how many mg/mL and how many g in a 500 mL bottle?*
5 % w/v = **50 mg/mL**; in 500 mL = **25 g of dextrose** ✔

---

## 6.4 ⭐⭐ DILUTION & CONCENTRATION

📌 **The principle of dilution:** *when a preparation is diluted, the **amount of active ingredient
stays the same** — only the total quantity changes.*

🔢 **THE UNIVERSAL DILUTION EQUATION**
```
          Q₁ × C₁  =  Q₂ × C₂
```
where Q = quantity (g or mL) and C = concentration (% or ratio strength).
🧠 **"Strong × its quantity = Weak × its quantity."**

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 9**
*How much water must be added to 250 mL of a 10 % w/v solution to make it 4 % w/v?*
`Q₂ = (250 × 10)/4 = 625 mL final volume` → **water to add = 625 − 250 = 375 mL** ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 10**
*What is the strength when 100 mL of 1 : 200 solution is diluted to 500 mL?*
1 : 200 = 0.5 % → `C₂ = (100 × 0.5)/500 = 0.1 %` = **1 : 1000** ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 11 (concentrating)**
*How much of a 95 % v/v alcohol is needed to prepare 1 litre of 70 % v/v alcohol?*
`Q₁ = (1000 × 70)/95 = 736.8 mL` of 95 % alcohol, then **water to 1000 mL** ✔
⚠️ **Note:** because alcohol & water **contract on mixing**, you must **make up to volume**, not
simply add 263 mL of water.

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 12 (dilution of a solid/ ointment)**
*How much white soft paraffin must be added to 30 g of a 10 % w/w sulphur ointment to reduce it to
2 % w/w?*
`Q₂ = (30 × 10)/2 = 150 g` → **paraffin to add = 150 − 30 = 120 g** ✔

### ⭐ DILUTION OF ALCOHOL & "PROOF SPIRIT"

📌 **Proof spirit** — the old British standard: a spirit which at **51 °F (10.6 °C)** weighs
**exactly 12/13ths of an equal volume of water**.
🔢 **Proof spirit contains ⭐ 57.1 % v/v (49.24 % w/w) of absolute alcohol.**
* **"Over proof (O.P.)"** = stronger than proof; **"Under proof (U.P.)"** = weaker than proof.
* 🔢 **100 volumes of proof spirit = 57.1 volumes of absolute alcohol.**
* 🔢 **Absolute alcohol = 100 % = 175.25° proof (i.e. 75.25 % O.P.).**
* 🔢 **Rectified spirit = 90 % v/v alcohol** (IP); **Absolute alcohol = 99.5 % v/v**;
  **Industrial methylated spirit = 95 % alcohol + 5 % wood naphtha**;
  **Surgical spirit** = methylated spirit + castor oil + methyl salicylate + diethyl phthalate.
* 🔢 **Dilute alcohols of the IP: 90 %, 70 %, 45 %, 25 %, 20 % v/v.**

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 13**
*Convert 70 % v/v alcohol into degrees proof.*
`Degrees proof = (% v/v × 100)/57.1 = (70 × 100)/57.1 = 122.6° proof` → i.e. **22.6 % over proof** ✔

🔢 **Percentage w/w ↔ v/v for alcohol** (needs specific gravity):
```
% v/v = (% w/w × sp. gr. of the solution) ÷ sp. gr. of absolute alcohol (0.7936)
```

---

## 6.5 ⭐⭐ ALLIGATION

📌 **ALLIGATION** is an arithmetical method of solving problems that involve **mixing two or more
preparations of different strengths** of the **same** substance.

### A. ALLIGATION MEDIAL
📌 **Alligation medial** is used to find the **strength (average/ mean) of a MIXTURE** made by
mixing **known quantities of known strengths**.
🔢 **Method:** multiply each quantity by its strength, add the products, divide by the total
quantity.

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 14**
*What is the strength of a mixture of 300 mL of 40 % w/v, 200 mL of 20 % w/v and 500 mL of 10 % w/v
solutions?*
```
300 × 40 = 12 000
200 × 20 =  4 000
500 × 10 =  5 000
Total   1000 mL   21 000
Strength = 21 000 ÷ 1000 = 21 % w/v ✔
```

### B. ALLIGATION ALTERNATE ⭐⭐
📌 **Alligation alternate** is used to find the **PROPORTION in which two (or more) preparations of
known strength must be mixed** to obtain a preparation of a **required intermediate strength**.

⭐ **THE CROSS (X) METHOD — the classic layout**
```
     Higher strength (H)              (D − L) parts of the HIGHER
                        ╲          ╱
                         Desired (D)
                        ╱          ╲
     Lower strength (L)               (H − D) parts of the LOWER
```
🔢 **Rule:** *Write the higher and lower strengths on the left, the desired strength in the middle;
subtract **diagonally** (always smaller from larger); the difference obtained on each diagonal is
the number of parts of the strength on the **same row**.*

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 15**
*In what proportion must 20 % and 5 % ointments be mixed to make a 10 % ointment?*
```
     20 %                     (10 − 5) = 5 parts of 20 %
          ╲              ╱
            10 %
          ╱              ╲
      5 %                     (20 − 10) = 10 parts of 5 %
```
→ **Ratio = 5 : 10 = 1 : 2** → mix **1 part of the 20 % with 2 parts of the 5 %.** ✔
*Check (alligation medial):* (1×20 + 2×5)/3 = 30/3 = 10 % ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 16 (with a total quantity)**
*Prepare 900 g of a 12 % w/w ointment from 25 % and 5 % ointments.*
```
     25 %              (12 − 5)  =  7 parts of 25 %
          ╲        ╱
            12 %
          ╱        ╲
      5 %              (25 − 12) = 13 parts of  5 %
   Total = 7 + 13 = 20 parts
```
* 25 % ointment = (7/20) × 900 = **315 g**
* 5 % ointment = (13/20) × 900 = **585 g** ✔ (total 900 g)

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 17 (diluent = 0 %)**
*How much white soft paraffin (0 %) must be mixed with a 20 % ointment to make 500 g of a 6 %
ointment?*
```
     20 %              (6 − 0)  =  6 parts of 20 %
          ╲        ╱
             6 %
          ╱        ╲
      0 %              (20 − 6) = 14 parts of 0 % (base)
   Total = 20 parts
```
* 20 % ointment = (6/20) × 500 = **150 g**
* Paraffin base = (14/20) × 500 = **350 g** ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 18 (100 % as one component)**
*In what proportion must pure (100 %) salicylic acid be mixed with a 2 % ointment to make a 10 %
ointment?*
```
    100 %              (10 − 2) =  8 parts of 100 % (pure acid)
          ╲        ╱
            10 %
          ╱        ╲
      2 %              (100 − 10) = 90 parts of 2 % ointment
```
→ **8 : 90 = 4 : 45** ✔

⚠️ **TRAP** — Alligation alternate works **only if the desired strength lies BETWEEN the two
available strengths**. You cannot make a 30 % ointment from a 20 % and a 5 % ointment.

---


## 6.6 ⭐⭐ HOW TO CALCULATE DOSES

### 6.6.1 Basic dose arithmetic

🔢 **THE THREE WORKHORSE FORMULAE**
```
① Number of doses  =  Total quantity ÷ Size of one dose

② Total quantity   =  Number of doses × Size of one dose

③ Size of one dose =  Total quantity ÷ Number of doses
```

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 19**
*How many 5 mL doses are there in a 150 mL bottle, and how long will it last at t.d.s.?*
`150 ÷ 5 = 30 doses`; at 3 doses/day → `30 ÷ 3 = 10 days` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 20**
*A patient must take 250 mg t.d.s. for 7 days. How many 125 mg tablets are needed?*
Per dose = 250/125 = 2 tablets; per day = 2 × 3 = 6; for 7 days = **42 tablets** ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 21 (drug in a volume of mixture)**
*A mixture contains 300 mg of a drug in each 10 mL dose. How much drug is in the 200 mL bottle?*
`(300 mg ÷ 10 mL) × 200 mL = 6000 mg = 6 g` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 22 (the classic "dose from a % solution")**
*How many mL of a 2 % w/v solution will provide a 40 mg dose?*
2 % w/v = 20 mg/mL → `40 ÷ 20 = 2 mL` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 23 (injection strength)**
*An ampoule is labelled "gentamicin 80 mg in 2 mL". How many mL give 60 mg?*
`(60/80) × 2 = 1.5 mL` ✔

🔢 **THE UNIVERSAL "DOSE FROM STOCK" FORMULA**
```
                    Dose required
Volume to give  =  ───────────────  ×  Volume of the stock
                   Stock strength
```

### 6.6.2 ⭐⭐ PAEDIATRIC (CHILD) DOSE FORMULAE — memorise all of them

| Formula | 🔢 Equation | Based on | When used |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⭐ **YOUNG'S RULE** | **Child dose = Adult dose × [ Age (yr) ÷ (Age + 12) ]** | **Age** | ⭐ Children **1–12 years** |
| ⭐ **DILLING'S RULE** | **Child dose = Adult dose × [ Age (yr) ÷ 20 ]** | **Age** | Children up to **20 years** |
| ⭐ **COWLING'S RULE** | **Child dose = Adult dose × [ (Age at next birthday) ÷ 24 ]** | **Age** | Children |
| ⭐ **FRIED'S RULE** | **Infant dose = Adult dose × [ Age in MONTHS ÷ 150 ]** | **Age in months** | ⭐ **Infants < 1–2 years** |
| ⭐ **CLARK'S RULE** | **Child dose = Adult dose × [ Weight in POUNDS ÷ 150 ]** | **Body weight** | ⭐ Any child (more reliable than age) |
| **Clark's (metric form)** | **Child dose = Adult dose × [ Weight in kg ÷ 68 ]** *(some texts 70)* | Body weight | — |
| ⭐ **BSA (Body Surface Area) RULE** | **Child dose = Adult dose × [ BSA of child (m²) ÷ 1.73 m² ]** | **Surface area** | ⭐ **The MOST ACCURATE method** — cytotoxics, neonates |
| **Catzel's / Percentage method** | Uses a table of % of adult dose against age/weight | — | Quick reference |
| **Bastedo's rule** | Child dose = Adult dose × [(Age + 3) ÷ 30] | Age | Older texts |
| **Augsberger's rule** | Child dose = Adult dose × [(4 × age in yr + 20) ÷ 100] | Age | Older texts |
| **Gaubius' rule** | Fractions by age (¼ at 4 yr, ⅓ at 7 yr, ½ at 14 yr…) | Age | Historic |
| **Body-weight (mg/kg) method** | **Dose = mg per kg × body weight (kg)** | Weight | ⭐ **The method actually used in modern practice** |

🔢 **NUMBER TO MEMORISE — the standard adult reference values:**
**Adult weight = 150 lb (≈ 68 kg or 70 kg); Adult BSA = 1.73 m²; Adult age reference = 20–24 years
(depending on the rule).**

🧠 **MEMORY HOOK for the denominators — "12, 20, 24, 150, 150, 1.73":**
**Young → +12** · **Dilling → 20** · **Cowling → 24** · **Fried → 150 (months)** ·
**Clark → 150 (pounds)** · **BSA → 1.73**.
🧠 "**F**ried is for **F**ants (in**F**ants) — in **months**; **C**lark uses **C**lark's **weight**
in pounds — both over **150**."

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 24 — Young's rule**
*Adult dose 500 mg; child aged 8 years.*
`500 × 8/(8 + 12) = 500 × 8/20 = 200 mg` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 25 — Dilling's rule**
*Adult dose 500 mg; child aged 8 years.*
`500 × 8/20 = 200 mg` ✔ *(coincides with Young's at 8 years)*

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 26 — Cowling's rule**
*Adult dose 600 mg; child is 5 years old (so next birthday = 6).*
`600 × 6/24 = 150 mg` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 27 — Fried's rule**
*Adult dose 300 mg; infant aged 9 months.*
`300 × 9/150 = 18 mg` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 28 — Clark's rule**
*Adult dose 400 mg; child weighs 50 lb.*
`400 × 50/150 = 133.3 mg` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 29 — BSA rule**
*Adult dose 750 mg; child's BSA = 0.62 m².*
`750 × 0.62/1.73 = 268.8 ≈ 269 mg` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 30 — mg/kg method**
*Amoxicillin 25 mg/kg/day in 3 divided doses for a 12 kg child; syrup is 125 mg/5 mL.*
Daily dose = 25 × 12 = 300 mg → per dose = 100 mg → volume = (100/125) × 5 = **4 mL t.d.s.** ✔

🔢 **BSA can be estimated** from **Du Bois** or **Mosteller's** formula:
```
Mosteller:  BSA (m²) = √[ height (cm) × weight (kg) ÷ 3600 ]
```
🔢 *Worked:* A child 100 cm tall weighing 16 kg → √(100 × 16/3600) = √0.444 = **0.67 m².**

⚠️ **TRAP** — Paediatric rules are **only approximations**. They must **NOT** be used for
**digoxin, cytotoxics, anticoagulants, aminoglycosides or anticonvulsants** — these are dosed on
**body weight or BSA with therapeutic drug monitoring**. Also, children are **not small adults**:
neonates have immature enzymes and **need proportionately smaller** doses, whereas older children
sometimes metabolise faster and need proportionately **larger** mg/kg doses.

### 6.6.3 ⭐ GERIATRIC & OTHER DOSE ADJUSTMENTS
* ⭐ Elderly patients generally need a **reduced dose** (reduced renal & hepatic function, lower
  lean body mass, altered receptor sensitivity) — often **½ to ¾** of the adult dose.
* **Renal impairment** → dose reduced in proportion to **creatinine clearance**
  🔢 **Cockcroft–Gault:** `CrCl = [(140 − age) × weight (kg)] ÷ (72 × serum creatinine mg/dL)`
  (× **0.85 for females**).
* **Obese patients** → dose on **ideal or adjusted body weight** for lipid-insoluble drugs.
* **Loading dose** 🔢 `= Vd × desired plasma concentration`;
  **Maintenance dose** 🔢 `= Clearance × desired concentration × dosing interval ÷ F`.

---

## 6.7 ISOTONIC SOLUTIONS ⭐

📌 **ISOTONIC** — a solution having the **same osmotic pressure as body fluids (blood, tears)**;
📌 **HYPOTONIC** — lower osmotic pressure → water enters cells → **haemolysis** of red cells;
📌 **HYPERTONIC** — higher osmotic pressure → water leaves cells → **crenation/ shrinkage**.

🔢 **NUMBERS TO MEMORISE**

| Reference | Value |
|---|---|
| ⭐ **Isotonic sodium chloride (normal saline)** | **0.9 % w/v NaCl** |
| ⭐ **Isotonic dextrose** | **5 % w/v** |
| ⭐ **Isotonic boric acid** | **1.9 % w/v** |
| ⭐ **Isotonic sodium bicarbonate** | 1.39 % w/v |
| ⭐ **Freezing point of blood/ tears** | **− 0.52 °C** |
| ⭐ **Osmotic pressure of blood** | ≈ **7 atm**; osmolarity ≈ **285–310 mOsm/L** |
| **pH of blood / tears** | 7.4 / 7.4 |
| ⭐ **NaCl equivalent of NaCl itself (E value)** | **1.0** |

📌 **SODIUM CHLORIDE EQUIVALENT (E value)** — the **weight of sodium chloride that produces the
same osmotic effect as 1 g of the drug**.

🔢 **METHOD 1 — THE SODIUM CHLORIDE EQUIVALENT METHOD**
```
NaCl required (g) = [ 0.9 × Volume (mL) ÷ 100 ]  −  ( Weight of drug × its E value )
```
🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 31**
*Make 100 mL of an isotonic solution of 1 % w/v atropine sulphate (E = 0.13).*
* NaCl needed for 100 mL isotonic = 0.9 g
* Osmotic contribution of the drug = 1 g × 0.13 = 0.13 g of "NaCl equivalent"
* **NaCl to add = 0.9 − 0.13 = 0.77 g** ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 32**
*30 mL of a 2 % w/v solution of a drug whose E = 0.20.*
* NaCl for isotonicity of 30 mL = (0.9 × 30)/100 = 0.27 g
* Drug present = (2 × 30)/100 = 0.6 g → NaCl equivalent = 0.6 × 0.20 = 0.12 g
* **NaCl to add = 0.27 − 0.12 = 0.15 g** ✔

🔢 **METHOD 2 — THE FREEZING-POINT (CRYOSCOPIC) METHOD**
```
                    0.52  −  ( ΔTf of the drug at its given % )
% w/v of NaCl  =  ───────────────────────────────────────────────
                        0.576   (the ΔTf of 1 % w/v NaCl)
```
🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 33**
*A 1 % solution of a drug lowers the freezing point by 0.15 °C. What % of NaCl is needed to make a
1 % solution of this drug isotonic?*
`(0.52 − 0.15)/0.576 = 0.37/0.576 = 0.64 % w/v NaCl` ✔

🔢 **METHOD 3 — WHITE–VINCENT METHOD** (volume to make the drug isotonic, then dilute with an
isotonic vehicle)
```
V = W × E × 111.1
```
where V = volume (mL) of isotonic solution that can be made from W grams of the drug.
🔢 *Worked:* 0.3 g of a drug with E = 0.20 → V = 0.3 × 0.20 × 111.1 = **6.67 mL** of isotonic
solution; make the drug up to 6.67 mL with water, then dilute to the final volume with an
**isotonic diluting vehicle** (e.g. 0.9 % NaCl or Sörensen's buffer).

⭐ **Substances used to adjust tonicity:** **sodium chloride, dextrose, boric acid, sodium
bicarbonate, sodium citrate, potassium nitrate, mannitol, propylene glycol.**
⚠️ **Boric acid (1.9 %)** is preferred for **eye drops of alkaloidal salts, silver salts and
zinc salts** — because sodium chloride would **precipitate** them.

---

## 6.8 MILLIEQUIVALENTS (mEq) & ELECTROLYTE CALCULATIONS ⭐

📌 An **equivalent weight** = **atomic (or molecular) weight ÷ valency**.
📌 **1 milliequivalent (mEq)** = **1/1000 of a gram-equivalent weight**.

🔢 **THE FORMULAE**
```
                         Atomic / Molecular weight (mg)
Weight of 1 mEq (mg) =  ────────────────────────────────
                                  Valency

           Weight of substance (mg) × Valency
mEq  =  ────────────────────────────────────────
              Molecular weight (mg)
```

🔢 **STANDARD VALUES TO MEMORISE**

| Ion / salt | Atomic/ Mol. wt | Valency | **1 mEq =** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Na⁺** | 23 | 1 | **23 mg** |
| **K⁺** | 39 | 1 | **39 mg** |
| **Ca²⁺** | 40 | 2 | **20 mg** |
| **Mg²⁺** | 24 | 2 | **12 mg** |
| **Cl⁻** | 35.5 | 1 | **35.5 mg** |
| **HCO₃⁻** | 61 | 1 | **61 mg** |
| **NaCl** | 58.5 | 1 | ⭐ **58.5 mg** |
| **KCl** | 74.5 | 1 | **74.5 mg** |
| **NaHCO₃** | 84 | 1 | ⭐ **84 mg** |
| **CaCl₂** | 111 | 2 | **55.5 mg** |

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 34**
*How many mEq of Na⁺ are in 1 litre of 0.9 % w/v sodium chloride?*
0.9 % w/v = 9 g NaCl per litre = 9000 mg;
`mEq = 9000 ÷ 58.5 = 153.8 ≈ 154 mEq of Na⁺ (and 154 mEq of Cl⁻)` ✔
⭐ **Remember: normal saline contains ≈ 154 mEq/L each of Na⁺ and Cl⁻.**

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 35**
*How many mg of KCl provide 20 mEq of potassium?*
`20 × 74.5 = 1490 mg = 1.49 g` ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 36**
*Express 4.2 % w/v sodium bicarbonate in mEq/L.*
4.2 % = 42 g/L = 42 000 mg → `42 000 ÷ 84 = 500 mEq/L` ✔

🔢 **OSMOLARITY**
```
                          weight (g/L) × 1000 × number of ions
mOsmol/L  =  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
                          molecular weight (g)
```
🔢 *Worked:* 0.9 % NaCl → (9 × 1000 × 2)/58.5 = **308 mOsmol/L** ✔ (isotonic ≈ 285–310).

---

## 6.9 ⭐ ENLARGEMENT & REDUCTION OF FORMULAE

📌 Official formulae are usually given **for 1000 mL or 1000 g**. To dispense a different quantity
you must **scale the formula by a FACTOR**.

🔢 **THE FACTOR METHOD**
```
             Quantity required
Factor  =  ─────────────────────   ; then multiply EVERY ingredient by the factor
           Quantity in the formula
```

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 37**
*The official formula of Compound Powder of Tragacanth makes 100 g:
Tragacanth 15 g, Acacia 20 g, Starch 20 g, Sucrose 45 g. Prepare 25 g.*
Factor = 25/100 = **0.25** →
Tragacanth **3.75 g**, Acacia **5 g**, Starch **5 g**, Sucrose **11.25 g** (total 25 g) ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 38**
*A formula for 1000 mL of a mixture contains Ammonium chloride 100 g, Liquorice liquid extract
50 mL, Chloroform water to 1000 mL. Prepare 150 mL.*
Factor = 150/1000 = **0.15** →
Ammonium chloride **15 g**, Liquorice liquid extract **7.5 mL**, Chloroform water **to 150 mL** ✔

🔢 **WORKED EXAMPLE 39 — formula given in "parts"**
*A formula reads: Zinc oxide 1 part, Starch 1 part, White soft paraffin 2 parts. Make 60 g.*
Total parts = 4 → 1 part = 60/4 = 15 g →
Zinc oxide **15 g**, Starch **15 g**, White soft paraffin **30 g** ✔

⭐ **RULES for enlargement/ reduction**
1. Apply the factor to **every** ingredient, including the vehicle if it is a fixed quantity.
2. If the vehicle is written as **"ad" (to make up to)**, do **not** multiply it — simply
   **make up to the new final volume**.
3. ⭐ **Always prepare a slight excess** (about **5–10 %**) of a liquid preparation to allow for
   losses in transfer & filtration — but **never of a potent/ narcotic drug**.
4. After scaling, **check that any quantity below the MWQ** is handled by the **aliquot method**
   (Ch. 4).
5. Round sensibly, but **never round a potent drug upward**.

---

## 6.10 DISPLACEMENT VALUE (an introduction — full treatment in Chapter 9)

📌 **DISPLACEMENT VALUE (DV)** — the **number of parts by weight of a drug that displaces
1 part by weight of the suppository base** (usually theobroma oil).

🔢 **THE WORKING FORMULA**
```
                        Weight of the drug in all the suppositories
Weight of base = (n × mould calibration) − ────────────────────────────
                                                Displacement value
```
🔢 **Quick example:** 12 suppositories of 1 g each containing 300 mg zinc oxide (DV = 4.7):
Total drug = 3.6 g; base displaced = 3.6/4.7 = 0.766 g;
Base required = 12 − 0.766 = **11.23 g**. ✔ *(Full worked treatment in Ch. 9.)*

---

## 6.11 MISCELLANEOUS CALCULATIONS THE PAPER LIKES

### A. Specific gravity ⭐
🔢 `Sp. gr. = weight of the substance ÷ weight of an equal volume of water`
🔢 **Weight (g) = Volume (mL) × Sp. gr.** and **Volume (mL) = Weight (g) ÷ Sp. gr.**

| Liquid | Sp. gr. |
|---|---|
| **Water** | **1.000** |
| **Alcohol (ethanol, absolute)** | **0.79** |
| **Rectified spirit (90 %)** | 0.834 |
| **Glycerin** | ⭐ **1.26** |
| **Chloroform** | ⭐ **1.47–1.48** |
| **Ether** | 0.72 |
| **Liquid paraffin** | 0.83–0.89 |
| **Castor oil** | 0.945–0.965 |
| **Olive/ arachis oil** | 0.91–0.92 |
| **Syrup (simple)** | ⭐ **1.315–1.33** |
| **Clove oil** | 1.04–1.06 (heavier than water) |
| **Mercury** | 13.6 |

🔢 *Worked:* What volume is occupied by 100 g of glycerin? `100 ÷ 1.26 = 79.4 mL` ✔
🔢 *Worked:* What is the weight of 30 mL of chloroform? `30 × 1.48 = 44.4 g` ✔

### B. Percentage of alcohol in a finished preparation
🔢 *A 200 mL mixture contains 30 mL of a tincture of 60 % v/v alcohol. What is the alcohol
percentage of the mixture?*
Absolute alcohol = 30 × 60/100 = 18 mL → `(18/200) × 100 = 9 % v/v` ✔

### C. Percentage & absolute error
🔢 `% error = (error ÷ quantity desired) × 100`
🔢 *Worked:* You intended 500 mg and actually weighed 520 mg → error 20 mg →
`(20/500) × 100 = 4 %` → **acceptable** (< 5 %). ✔

### D. Number of doses/ days' supply with a changing schedule
🔢 *A 100 mL bottle: 10 mL b.d. for 3 days, then 5 mL b.d. How long will it last?*
First 3 days use 20 × 3 = 60 mL; remaining 40 mL at 10 mL/day = 4 days → **total 7 days** ✔

### E. Drip-rate calculation (hospital dispensing) ⭐
```
                      Volume (mL) × Drop factor (drops/mL)
Drops per minute = ────────────────────────────────────────
                              Time in minutes
```
🔢 *Worked:* 500 mL over 4 hours with a 20 drops/mL set →
`(500 × 20)/(4 × 60) = 10 000/240 = 41.7 ≈ 42 drops/min` ✔
⭐ Standard drop factors: **macro-drip 15 or 20 drops/mL; micro-drip (paediatric) 60 drops/mL**.

### F. Reducing/ increasing a potency-based formula (units)
🔢 *Worked:* Insulin 100 units/mL — how many mL give 28 units? `28/100 = 0.28 mL` ✔

### G. Proof spirit & duty (classical)
🔢 *Worked:* How many mL of absolute alcohol are in 500 mL of proof spirit?
`500 × 57.1/100 = 285.5 mL` ✔

---

## 6.12 CHAPTER 6 — RAPID REVISION SHEET (the numbers you must be able to recite)

**METRIC**
* 1 kg = 1000 g; 1 g = 1000 mg; 1 mg = 1000 µg; 1 L = 1000 mL; **1 mL = 1 cc**;
  **1 mL of water = 1 g**.

**APOTHECARY**
* **20 grains = 1 scruple · 3 scruples = 1 drachm (60 gr) · 8 drachms = 1 ounce (480 gr) ·
  12 ounces = 1 pound (5760 gr)**
* **60 minims = 1 fl drachm · 8 fl drachms = 1 fl ounce · 20 fl oz = 1 pint · 8 pints = 1 gallon**

**CONVERSIONS**
* **1 grain = 64.8 mg (≈ 65 mg)** · **1 g = 15.43 grains (≈ 15)**
* **1 minim = 0.06 mL** · **1 mL = 16.9 ≈ 17 minims**
* **1 fl drachm = 3.55 mL (≈ 4)** · **1 fl oz = 28.4 mL (≈ 30)**
* **1 apoth. ounce = 31.1 g** · **1 avoirdupois ounce = 28.35 g**
* **1 avoirdupois pound = 453.6 g** · **1 troy pound = 373.2 g** · **1 kg = 2.2 lb**
* **1 Imperial pint = 568 mL** · **1 US pint = 473 mL** · **1 Imperial gallon = 4546 mL**
* **1 inch = 2.54 cm**
* **°F = (9/5 °C) + 32 · °C = 5/9 (°F − 32) · K = °C + 273 · both scales equal at −40°**
* **Body temp 37 °C = 98.6 °F**

**PERCENTAGE**
* **% w/w** = g in 100 g · **% w/v** = g in 100 mL · **% v/v** = mL in 100 mL ·
  **% v/w** = mL in 100 g.
* Solid in liquid → **w/v**; liquid in liquid → **v/v**; solid/semisolid mixtures → **w/w**.
* **1 % w/v = 10 mg/mL** · **1 : 1000 = 0.1 %** · **1 ppm = 0.0001 % = 1 mg/L = 1 µg/mL**.
* **Ratio 1 : x → % = 100/x** and **% p → ratio 1 : (100/p)**.

**DILUTION & ALLIGATION**
* **Q₁C₁ = Q₂C₂.**
* **Alligation medial** → find the **strength of a mixture**;
  **alligation alternate** → find the **proportion** to mix (cross/ X method, subtract diagonally).
* Desired strength must lie **between** the two available strengths.

**ALCOHOL**
* **Proof spirit = 57.1 % v/v alcohol**; absolute alcohol = **175.25° proof**;
  **rectified spirit = 90 % v/v**; absolute alcohol = 99.5 % v/v.
* `Degrees proof = (% v/v × 100) ÷ 57.1`.

**DOSES**
* **Young's = A × age/(age + 12)** · **Dilling's = A × age/20** ·
  **Cowling's = A × (age next birthday)/24** · **Fried's = A × months/150** ·
  **Clark's = A × lb/150 (or kg/68)** · **BSA = A × child BSA/1.73**.
* Adult reference: **150 lb (68–70 kg), BSA 1.73 m²**.
* **Mosteller BSA = √(ht cm × wt kg ÷ 3600)**.
* **Cockcroft–Gault CrCl = [(140 − age) × wt] ÷ (72 × S.Cr)**, × 0.85 for women.
* **Volume to give = (dose required ÷ stock strength) × stock volume.**

**ISOTONICITY**
* **0.9 % NaCl · 5 % dextrose · 1.9 % boric acid** are isotonic;
  **freezing point of blood = −0.52 °C**; ΔTf of 1 % NaCl = **0.576**.
* **NaCl to add = (0.9 × volume/100) − (weight of drug × E value)**.
* **White–Vincent: V = W × E × 111.1.**
* Hypotonic → **haemolysis**; hypertonic → **crenation**.

**mEq**
* **1 mEq = mol. wt ÷ valency (in mg)**; **NaCl 58.5 mg · NaHCO₃ 84 mg · KCl 74.5 mg ·
  Na⁺ 23 · K⁺ 39 · Ca²⁺ 20 · Mg²⁺ 12 · Cl⁻ 35.5 mg.**
* **Normal saline ≈ 154 mEq/L of Na⁺ and Cl⁻; 308 mOsmol/L.**

**OTHERS**
* **Factor = quantity required ÷ quantity in the formula**; do not multiply an "ad" vehicle.
* **Weight = volume × sp. gr.**; glycerin 1.26, chloroform 1.48, alcohol 0.79, syrup 1.32.
* **% error = (error ÷ desired quantity) × 100**; ≤ 5 % is acceptable.
* **Drops/min = (volume × drop factor) ÷ minutes**; macro 15–20, micro 60 drops/mL.
* **Displacement value:** base = (n × mould size) − (total drug ÷ DV).

# CHAPTER 7
# 💉 DOSES OF DRUGS & PHARMACOPOEIAL PREPARATIONS WITH THEIR DOSES

---

## 7.1 POSOLOGY & THE VOCABULARY OF DOSE

📌 **POSOLOGY** — the branch of medical/ pharmaceutical science that deals with **the dose of a
drug** (Gk. *posos* = how much + *logos* = study).

📌 **DOSE** — the **quantity of a drug or medicine prescribed to be taken at one time, or in a
stated period, to produce the desired therapeutic effect** in a patient of average build & age.

### ⭐⭐ TYPES OF DOSE — the master definition table

| Type of dose | 📌 Definition |
|---|---|
| **Single dose** | The quantity given **at one time** |
| **Daily dose** | The total quantity given in **24 hours** |
| **Total (course) dose** | The quantity given over the **whole course** of treatment |
| **Divided (fractional) dose** | The daily dose split into **smaller doses at intervals** |
| ⭐ **Minimum (threshold) dose** | The **smallest** amount that will produce a **detectable therapeutic effect** |
| ⭐ **Maximum dose** | The **largest** amount that can be given **without producing toxic effects** — ⚠️ the pharmacist must check every prescription against this |
| ⭐ **Therapeutic (usual/ medicinal) dose** | The dose that produces the **desired therapeutic effect** — lies between the minimum and the maximum |
| ⭐ **Toxic dose** | The amount that produces **harmful/ poisonous effects** |
| ⭐ **Lethal (fatal) dose** | The amount that causes **death** |
| ⭐ **LOADING (priming/ initial) dose** | A **large initial dose** given to achieve the **effective plasma concentration rapidly**; e.g. digoxin, sulphonamides, antimalarials, some antibiotics |
| ⭐ **MAINTENANCE dose** | The dose given **repeatedly** to **maintain** the therapeutic plasma level |
| **Prophylactic dose** | Given to **prevent** a disease (e.g. antimalarial prophylaxis, tetanus toxoid) |
| **Diagnostic dose** | Given as an aid to **diagnosis** (e.g. barium meal, radio-opaque contrast, tuberculin, histamine test) |
| **Booster dose** | A later dose to **reinforce immunity** |
| **Depot dose** | A single dose from a **long-acting (depot) preparation** giving slow, sustained release |
| **Standard/ Official dose** | The dose stated in the **pharmacopoeia** |
| ⭐ **ED₅₀ (median effective dose)** | Dose producing the desired effect in **50 %** of the population |
| ⭐ **LD₅₀ (median lethal dose)** | Dose that kills **50 %** of the test animals — a measure of **acute toxicity** |
| ⭐ **TD₅₀** | Dose producing toxicity in **50 %** |
| ⭐ **THERAPEUTIC INDEX (TI)** | 🔢 **TI = LD₅₀ ÷ ED₅₀** — a measure of the **safety margin** of a drug. ⭐ **The HIGHER the TI, the SAFER the drug.** ⚠️ **Low TI (narrow margin) drugs: digoxin, lithium, warfarin, phenytoin, theophylline, aminoglycosides, cytotoxics** — these need therapeutic drug monitoring |
| **Certain Safety Factor (CSF)** | 🔢 **LD₁ ÷ ED₉₉** — a stricter safety measure |
| **Margin of safety** | The range between the therapeutic and the toxic dose |

🧠 **THE DOSE LADDER — memorise the order:**
**Minimum (threshold) → Therapeutic → Maximum → Toxic → Lethal.**

---

## 7.2 ⭐⭐ FACTORS AFFECTING THE DOSE OF A DRUG

📌 A very frequently asked "list" question. Learn the **five groups**.

### GROUP A — Factors relating to the PATIENT

| Factor | Effect on dose |
|---|---|
| ⭐ **Age** | **Children need less** (immature enzymes, kidneys & blood–brain barrier); the **elderly need less** (reduced renal/ hepatic function). Use the paediatric formulae (Ch. 6) |
| ⭐ **Body weight & body surface area** | Dose ∝ weight/ BSA; obese & very thin patients need adjustment. **BSA is the most accurate basis** |
| ⭐ **Sex** | **Women generally need a slightly smaller dose** (lower body weight, higher proportion of fat); ⚠️ **special caution in pregnancy, lactation & menstruation** — teratogens, drugs excreted in milk, ecbolics & purgatives are avoided |
| **Species & race** | Genetic differences in metabolism (e.g. **slow vs fast acetylators of isoniazid**) |
| ⭐ **Idiosyncrasy** | ⭐ An **abnormal, unpredictable, genetically determined** reaction to a normal dose (e.g. **haemolysis with primaquine in G6PD deficiency**, malignant hyperthermia with halothane) |
| ⭐ **Allergy / hypersensitivity** | An immunologically mediated reaction (penicillin anaphylaxis) — **dose-independent** |
| ⭐ **Tolerance** | ⭐ **Progressively larger doses needed to produce the same effect** on repeated use — e.g. **morphine, barbiturates, alcohol, nitrates, ephedrine**. **Tachyphylaxis** = rapid tolerance after a few doses (ephedrine, amphetamine) |
| ⭐ **Cumulation** | ⭐ The drug is **eliminated more slowly than it is administered**, so it **accumulates** to toxic levels — e.g. **digitalis, heavy metals, emetine, bromides, chloroquine** |
| ⭐ **Habituation & addiction (drug dependence)** | Psychological/ physical dependence — morphine, barbiturates, alcohol |
| **Pathological state / disease** | ⚠️ **Liver disease** → reduced metabolism → **reduce dose**; ⚠️ **Kidney disease** → reduced excretion → **reduce dose**; fever, dehydration, hypoproteinaemia, thyroid state, GI disease (absorption) all alter dose |
| **Emotional/ psychological state** | Placebo response; anxiety alters requirement of analgesics & sedatives |
| **Genetic factors (pharmacogenetics)** | Enzyme polymorphisms (CYP2D6, G6PD, pseudocholinesterase) |
| **Diet & nutrition** | Food may ↓ or ↑ absorption; grapefruit juice inhibits CYP3A4; protein-rich diet ↑ metabolism |
| **Circadian rhythm (chronopharmacology)** | Corticosteroids best in the **morning**; antihistamines & hypnotics at **night** |

### GROUP B — Factors relating to the DRUG

| Factor | Effect |
|---|---|
| ⭐ **Route of administration** | ⭐ **Parenteral (IV) doses are SMALLER than oral doses** because oral drugs undergo **incomplete absorption + first-pass metabolism**. e.g. morphine oral 10–30 mg but IM 10 mg |
| ⭐ **Dosage form** | Solutions act faster than suspensions > capsules > tablets > coated/ SR tablets |
| **Physicochemical properties** | Particle size, solubility, polymorphic form, salt form, pKa, lipid solubility, degree of ionisation |
| **Bioavailability** | 🔢 **F = fraction of the administered dose reaching the systemic circulation unchanged**; IV F = 1 (100 %) |
| **Dose frequency & interval** | Determined by the **half-life (t½)** |
| **Purity & potency of the preparation** | Standardised/ assayed preparations only |
| **Age & storage of the preparation** | A deteriorated drug is under-potent |

### GROUP C — Factors relating to DRUG INTERACTION & COMBINATION
* ⭐ **Synergism** — the combined effect is **greater** than the sum of the individual effects.
  * **Additive (summation)** — 1 + 1 = 2, e.g. **aspirin + paracetamol**.
  * **True (super-additive) synergism/ potentiation** — 1 + 1 > 2, e.g.
    **sulphamethoxazole + trimethoprim**, **levodopa + carbidopa**,
    **penicillin + probenecid**, **adrenaline + local anaesthetic**.
* ⭐ **Antagonism** — one drug **opposes** the effect of another.
  * **Chemical antagonism** — they react chemically (**tannic acid + alkaloid**,
    **chelation: dimercaprol + arsenic; desferrioxamine + iron**).
  * **Physical antagonism** — adsorption (**activated charcoal + a poison**).
  * **Physiological/ functional antagonism** — opposite effects through **different receptors**
    (**adrenaline vs histamine**).
  * **Pharmacological/ competitive antagonism** — both act on the **same receptor**
    (**atropine vs acetylcholine; naloxone vs morphine; propranolol vs adrenaline**).
* ⭐ **Enzyme induction** → faster metabolism → **larger dose needed** —
  **phenobarbitone, phenytoin, rifampicin, carbamazepine, griseofulvin, smoking, alcohol
  (chronic)**.
* ⭐ **Enzyme inhibition** → slower metabolism → **smaller dose needed / toxicity** —
  **cimetidine, erythromycin, ketoconazole, metronidazole, allopurinol, isoniazid,
  chloramphenicol, grapefruit juice**.
* **Displacement from plasma protein binding** — aspirin displaces warfarin → bleeding.
* **Altered absorption** — antacids/ iron/ milk chelate tetracyclines & fluoroquinolones.
* **Altered renal excretion** — probenecid blocks penicillin secretion;
  alkalinisation of urine speeds salicylate excretion.

### GROUP D — Factors relating to the TIME & MANNER of administration
* **Before food (a.c.)** — for drugs whose absorption is reduced by food (ampicillin, tetracycline,
  levodopa) and for appetite stimulants/ bitters.
* **After food (p.c.)** — for **gastric irritants**: aspirin/ NSAIDs, iron, metronidazole,
  griseofulvin (fatty meal aids absorption), corticosteroids.
* **On an empty stomach/ at bedtime** — hypnotics, laxatives.
* **Duration of therapy & cumulative effect**; adherence/ compliance.

### GROUP E — ENVIRONMENTAL factors
Temperature & climate (sweating alters fluid & electrolyte requirement), altitude, occupational
exposure, smoking (**enzyme induction**), alcohol.

🧠 **MEMORY HOOK — the 12 classic "factors affecting dose" for a written answer:**
**A**ge, **B**ody weight/ BSA, **S**ex, **R**oute, **T**ime of administration, **T**olerance,
**I**diosyncrasy, **C**umulation, **D**isease (liver/kidney), **D**rug interaction/ synergism &
antagonism, **G**enetic factors, **E**nvironment.

---

## 7.3 ⭐ THE CLASSICAL DOSAGE FORMS & OFFICIAL PREPARATIONS — DEFINITIONS

📌 Learn each **definition + its official strength**, because the examiner asks both
*"Define a linctus"* and *"What is the strength of a fresh infusion?"*

### A. ORAL LIQUID PREPARATIONS

| Preparation | 📌 Definition | 🔢 Strength / dose |
|---|---|---|
| ⭐ **MIXTURE (Mistura)** | A **liquid oral preparation** containing one or more drugs **dissolved, suspended or dispersed** in a suitable vehicle, intended to be taken in **divided doses** | Dose usually **10–15 mL**; supplied 100–300 mL; ⚠️ **suspensions need "Shake well"** |
| ⭐ **DRAUGHT (Haustus)** | A mixture intended to be taken as a **SINGLE dose** | **Usually 50 mL** (some texts 30–50 mL) |
| ⭐ **SOLUTION (Solutio)** | A **clear, homogeneous, molecularly dispersed** liquid preparation of a solute in a solvent | — |
| ⭐ **SYRUP (Syrupus)** | A **concentrated, viscous, nearly saturated aqueous solution of sucrose** or another sugar; may be medicated or simply a flavoured vehicle | ⭐ **Simple Syrup IP = 66.7 % w/w sucrose** (≈ 85 % w/v; **667 g sucrose in 1000 g** of syrup). Sp. gr. ≈ **1.315–1.33**. Self-preserving because of high osmotic pressure |
| ⭐ **ELIXIR** | A **clear, sweetened, pleasantly flavoured HYDRO-ALCOHOLIC** liquid preparation for oral use | Alcohol 5–40 %; dose 5–15 mL. e.g. **Elixir of Piperazine Citrate, Paediatric Chloral Elixir** |
| ⭐ **LINCTUS** | ⭐ A **VISCOUS, SWEET, DEMULCENT** oral liquid used in the treatment of **COUGH**, to be **sipped slowly and taken UNDILUTED** so as to soothe the throat | Dose **5 mL** (⚠️ label: *"to be sipped slowly, undiluted"*). e.g. **Simple Linctus, Codeine Linctus, Squill Opiate Linctus** |
| ⭐ **TINCTURE (Tinctura)** | An **alcoholic or hydro-alcoholic solution** prepared from **vegetable drugs or chemical substances** | ⭐ **Simple/non-potent drugs = 1 in 5 (20 % w/v)**; ⭐ **potent drugs = 1 in 10 (10 % w/v)** |
| ⭐ **SPIRIT (Spiritus)** | An **alcoholic or hydro-alcoholic solution of a VOLATILE substance** | Usually **5–10 %**; e.g. **Spirit of Camphor 10 %, Aromatic Spirit of Ammonia, Spirit of Peppermint 10 %** |
| ⭐ **AROMATIC WATER (Aqua)** | A **clear, SATURATED aqueous solution of a volatile oil** or other aromatic/ volatile substance | ≈ **0.2 %**; e.g. **Peppermint Water, Chloroform Water (⭐ = 0.25 % v/v chloroform, i.e. Double-strength chloroform water diluted 1 : 1)**, Anise Water, Dill Water, Cinnamon Water |
| ⭐ **INFUSION (Infusum)** | A **dilute aqueous solution** of the readily soluble constituents of a crude drug, prepared by **macerating** the drug with **cold or boiling water for a SHORT time (15–30 min)** | ⭐ **Fresh infusion = 1 in 10 (10 % w/v); SHELF-LIFE 12 HOURS.** ⭐ **Concentrated infusion = 8× strength** (dilute **1 part with 7 parts water**), preserved with 20–25 % alcohol |
| ⭐ **DECOCTION (Decoctum)** | An aqueous preparation made by **BOILING** the drug with water (about 10 min) and **straining while hot** | Used for hard, woody drugs; very short shelf-life |
| ⭐ **LIQUID (FLUID) EXTRACT** | A **concentrated liquid preparation** of a vegetable drug, made so that **1 mL is equivalent to 1 g of the drug** | ⭐ **1 in 1**; e.g. Liquid Extract of Liquorice, of Belladonna, of Ipecacuanha, of Cascara |
| **DRY / SOFT EXTRACT** | The evaporated concentrate of a drug extract, of powder (dry) or ointment-like (soft) consistency | e.g. Belladonna Dry Extract |
| **EMULSION (Emulsio)** | A **biphasic** liquid preparation of two immiscible liquids, one dispersed as globules in the other, stabilised by an **emulgent** | ⭐ *"Shake well"*; e.g. **Liquid Paraffin Emulsion, Cod Liver Oil Emulsion, Castor Oil Emulsion** |
| **SUSPENSION** | A **biphasic** preparation of finely divided **insoluble solid** dispersed in a liquid | ⭐ *"Shake well"* |
| **MUCILAGE (Mucilago)** | A **thick, viscous, adhesive aqueous solution of a gum**, used as a suspending/ emulsifying agent | ⭐ **Mucilage of Acacia = 35 % w/v**; Mucilage of Tragacanth = **1.25 % w/v**; Mucilage of Starch ≈ 2 % |
| **GLYCERIN / GLYCERITE (Glycerinum)** | A solution of a medicament in **glycerin** | e.g. **Glycerin of Boric acid (11 % w/w), Glycerin of Starch, Glycerin of Thymol Compound, Glycerin of Tannic acid, Glycerin of Ichthammol** |
| **VINEGAR (Acetum)** | A solution of a drug in **dilute acetic acid** | e.g. Vinegar of Squill |
| **HONEY (Mel)** | A preparation with **honey** as the vehicle | e.g. **Honey of Borax (Borax + honey + glycerin)** — mouth paint for thrush |
| **OXYMEL** | A preparation of honey + acetic acid | Classical |
| **JULEP** | A sweetened, flavoured aqueous vehicle | Classical |
| **ORAL DROPS** | Concentrated liquid measured in drops | Paediatric vitamins |
| **ORAL POWDER / EFFERVESCENT GRANULES** | Dry preparation dissolved/ dispersed in water before use | e.g. **Effervescent Magnesium Sulphate, ORS** |

### B. PREPARATIONS FOR EXTERNAL APPLICATION

| Preparation | 📌 Definition | ⭐ Key point |
|---|---|---|
| ⭐ **LOTION (Lotio)** | A **liquid** preparation for **application to the skin WITHOUT FRICTION** (dabbed/ patted on, or applied on a dressing) | ⭐ RED label, "FOR EXTERNAL USE"; **often needs "Shake well"** (calamine lotion). Produces a **cooling** effect by evaporation |
| ⭐ **LINIMENT (Linimentum)** | A **liquid or semi-liquid** preparation for **application to the skin WITH FRICTION (rubbing)** | ⭐ Usually contains an oil/ soap/ alcohol; ⚠️ **NEVER applied to broken skin**; **counter-irritant**. Ribbed bottle, RED label |
| ⭐ **APPLICATION** | A liquid/ semi-liquid preparation applied to the skin, usually as a **parasiticide** | e.g. **Benzyl Benzoate Application (25 %)** for scabies |
| ⭐ **PAINT (Pigmentum)** | A liquid applied to the **skin or mucous membrane with a BRUSH**, leaving a film | e.g. **Compound Podophyllum Paint (15–25 %), Magenta Paint (Castellani's), Iodine Paint** |
| ⭐ **COLLODION (Collodium)** | A liquid preparation of **pyroxylin (nitrocellulose) in a mixture of ether & alcohol**, which on application **evaporates leaving a tough, flexible, protective film** | e.g. **Flexible Collodion (+ castor oil & colophony), Salicylic Acid Collodion (for corns/ warts)**. ⚠️ **Highly inflammable** |
| **CATAPLASM / POULTICE (Cataplasma)** | A **soft, moist, heated, paste-like** preparation applied to the skin to **retain heat** & relieve pain/ inflammation | e.g. **Kaolin Poultice, Linseed Poultice**; ⭐ applied **as hot as the patient can bear**, spread on a cloth |
| ⭐ **PLASTER (Emplastrum)** | A **solid or semisolid adhesive mass** spread on a **backing** (cloth/ plastic), which **adheres to the skin on warming** | See Ch. 9 |
| **DUSTING POWDER** | A finely divided powder for **external application** | ⭐ Must pass a **no. 120/ 180 sieve**; must NOT be applied to open wounds unless **sterile** |
| **EAR (Aural) DROPS** | Solution/ suspension instilled into the ear; vehicle = water, glycerin, propylene glycol, alcohol | e.g. Sodium bicarbonate ear drops |
| **NASAL DROPS / SPRAY** | Solution instilled into the nose; ⭐ vehicle should be **isotonic & near pH 5.5–7.5**, preferably **aqueous** (⚠️ **oily nasal drops may cause lipoid pneumonia**) | Ephedrine nasal drops 0.5–1 % |
| **EYE DROPS (Guttae ophthalmicae)** | ⭐ **STERILE, isotonic, buffered, particle-free** aqueous/ oily solution or suspension for the eye, containing a **preservative** (benzalkonium chloride 0.01 %, chlorhexidine, phenylmercuric nitrate 0.002 %) in multi-dose containers | ⭐ **Discard 4 weeks after opening**; single-dose units for eye surgery must be **preservative-free** |
| **EYE LOTION (Collyrium)** | A **sterile** aqueous solution for **bathing/ irrigating** the eye; ⭐ **must be preservative-free & discarded within 24 h of opening** | Sodium chloride eye lotion |
| **EYE OINTMENT (Oculentum)** | Sterile ointment for the eye, base = **Simple Eye Ointment Base (liquid paraffin 10 %, wool fat 10 %, yellow soft paraffin 80 %)** | Sterile |
| **GARGLE (Gargarisma)** | ⭐ An **aqueous solution used to treat the THROAT**, usually supplied **CONCENTRATED to be DILUTED with warm water before use** | ⭐ Labels: **"NOT TO BE SWALLOWED", "Dilute before use"** |
| **MOUTHWASH (Collutorium)** | An aqueous solution used for **rinsing the mouth** | "Not to be swallowed" |
| **THROAT PAINT** | Viscous (glycerin-based) liquid applied to the throat with a brush | **Compound Glycerin of Thymol, Mandl's paint** |
| **DOUCHE** | An aqueous solution directed **against a body cavity/ part** (eye, nose, throat, vagina) | Warm, isotonic |
| **ENEMA** | A liquid preparation given **rectally** | ⭐ **Evacuant enema** (e.g. **soap enema, glycerin, sodium phosphate**) — cleanses the bowel; ⭐ **Retention enema** (e.g. **arachis oil, corticosteroid, paraldehyde**) — held in for absorption or local effect. Given at **body temperature** |
| **SUPPOSITORY / PESSARY / BOUGIE** | See Ch. 9 | — |
| **INHALATION / NEBULA / SPRAY / INSUFFLATION** | See Ch. 11 | — |
| **OINTMENT / CREAM / PASTE / JELLY / GEL** | See Ch. 10 | — |

### C. SOLID ORAL DOSAGE FORMS

| Preparation | 📌 Definition | ⭐ Key point |
|---|---|---|
| ⭐ **POWDER (Pulvis)** | A **dry mixture of finely divided drugs** for internal or external use | **Bulk powder** (dispensed in a wide-mouth jar, dose measured by the patient — only for non-potent drugs like antacids & laxatives) vs **DIVIDED powder/ Chartula** (each dose wrapped separately in a powder paper — **essential for potent drugs**). Powder papers are folded by the **standard double-fold** method |
| **EFFERVESCENT POWDER/ GRANULES** | Contains an **acid (citric/ tartaric) + a bicarbonate**, which release CO₂ in water | ⚠️ Must be kept **absolutely dry, air-tight** |
| **CACHET** | A **rice-paper (starch) shell** enclosing an unpleasant powder; moistened & swallowed | Obsolete |
| **CAPSULE (Capsula)** | Drug enclosed in a **gelatin shell** — **hard (two-piece, for powders/ granules)** or **soft (one-piece, for liquids/ oils)** | ⭐ Hard capsule sizes: **000 (largest) → 5 (smallest)**; sizes 0, 1, 2 commonest |
| **TABLET (Tabella/ Compressi)** | A solid unit dosage form made by **compression** (or moulding) of powders/ granules | Types: uncoated, sugar-coated, film-coated, **enteric-coated**, effervescent, dispersible, chewable, sublingual, buccal, **sustained/ controlled release**, implant, vaginal |
| **PILL (Pilula)** | A **small, spherical mass** of drug + excipient, made by hand-rolling | Obsolete; excipients: liquorice powder, acacia, glucose, glycerin |
| **LOZENGE / TROCHE (Trochiscus)** | A **flavoured, hard, solid** dosage form to be **sucked slowly in the mouth** for a local effect on the mouth/ throat | Base = sugar + acacia |
| **PASTILLE (Pastillus)** | Like a lozenge but with a **soft, glycero-gelatin or glycerin–gelatin base** | Sucked slowly |
| **GRANULE** | Small agglomerated particles | For reconstitution |
| **IMPLANT / PELLET** | Sterile, small, sterile cylinder inserted subcutaneously for prolonged release | Hormones |
| **TABLET TRITURATE** | Small, moulded, rapidly soluble tablet made from a moist mass | For potent drugs |

### D. STERILE PREPARATIONS

| Preparation | 📌 Definition | ⭐ Key point |
|---|---|---|
| **INJECTION (Injectio)** | A **sterile, pyrogen-free** solution, suspension or emulsion, or a sterile powder for reconstitution, for **parenteral** administration | **SVP** (small volume, ≤ 100 mL) & ⭐ **LVP (large volume parenteral, > 100 mL — must be isotonic, pyrogen-free, and contain NO preservative or bacteriostat)** |
| **INFUSION (IV)** | Large-volume sterile aqueous solution given IV | Dextrose, saline, Ringer's |
| **IMPLANT** | Sterile pellet | — |
| **IRRIGATION** | Sterile solution for irrigating cavities | Not for injection |
| **DIALYSIS SOLUTION** | Sterile solution for dialysis | — |

---


## 7.4 ⭐⭐ MASTER DOSE TABLES OF PHARMACOPOEIAL PREPARATIONS

> ⚠️ **All doses are ADULT ORAL doses unless stated otherwise.** Values follow the classical
> IP/BP/BPC dispensing literature, because that is the source of the examination.
> Where editions differ, the most commonly printed value is given first.

### 📋 TABLE 7A — TINCTURES ⭐⭐

| Tincture | Strength | ⭐ Dose | Chief use |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Tincture of Belladonna** | 1 in 10 (potent) | **0.5 – 2 mL** | Antispasmodic, antisialagogue |
| **Tincture of Nux Vomica** | 1 in 10 | **0.5 – 2 mL** (max 2 mL) | Bitter tonic, CNS stimulant |
| **Tincture of Digitalis** | 1 in 10 | **0.3 – 1 mL** | Cardiotonic |
| **Tincture of Opium (Laudanum)** | 1 in 10 (≈ 1 % morphine) | ⭐ **0.3 – 2 mL** | Narcotic analgesic, antidiarrhoeal |
| **Camphorated Tincture of Opium (PAREGORIC)** | Weak (0.05 % morphine) | ⭐ **2 – 8 mL** | Antitussive, antidiarrhoeal (paediatric) |
| **Tincture of Ipecacuanha** | 1 in 10 | **0.25 – 2 mL** (emetic 15–30 mL) | Expectorant / emetic |
| **Tincture of Squill** | 1 in 5 | **0.6 – 2 mL** | Expectorant |
| **Tincture of Ginger (weak)** | 1 in 5 | **0.5 – 2 mL** | Carminative, flavour |
| **Strong Tincture of Ginger** | 1 in 2 | **0.25 – 0.5 mL** | Flavour |
| **Tincture of Orange (Sweet Orange Peel)** | 1 in 5 | **2 – 4 mL** | Flavouring |
| **Tincture of Lemon** | 1 in 5 | **0.5 – 2 mL** | Flavouring |
| **Compound Tincture of Cardamom** | — | **2 – 4 mL** | Carminative, flavour, colour |
| **Tincture of Cinchona (Compound)** | 1 in 8 | **2 – 4 mL** | Bitter tonic |
| **Tincture of Gentian (Compound)** | 1 in 10 | **2 – 4 mL** | Bitter, appetite stimulant |
| **Tincture of Quassia** | 1 in 10 | **2 – 4 mL** | Bitter, anthelmintic |
| **Tincture of Rhubarb (Compound)** | — | **2 – 4 mL** | Laxative, astringent |
| **Tincture of Senna** | 1 in 5 | **2 – 4 mL** | Purgative |
| **Tincture of Cascara Sagrada** | 1 in 5 | **2 – 4 mL** | Laxative |
| **Tincture of Myrrh** | 1 in 5 | **2 – 4 mL** (gargle: diluted) | Astringent, gargle |
| **Tincture of Benzoin (Simple)** | 1 in 5 | External | Antiseptic |
| ⭐ **Compound Tincture of Benzoin (Friar's Balsam)** | Benzoin 10, Storax 7.5, Tolu 2.5, Aloes 2.5, alcohol to 100 | ⭐ **Inhalation: 1 (–5) mL to 500 mL (1 pint) of hot water** | Inhalation, protective |
| **Tincture of Tolu** | 1 in 10 | **2 – 4 mL** | Expectorant |
| **Tincture of Capsicum** | 1 in 20 | **0.06 – 0.2 mL** | Counter-irritant, carminative |
| **Tincture of Aconite** | 1 in 10 | **0.06 – 0.2 mL** (⚠️ very potent) | Analgesic (obsolete) |
| **Tincture of Stramonium** | 1 in 10 | **0.5 – 2 mL** | Antispasmodic |
| **Tincture of Hyoscyamus** | 1 in 10 | **2 – 4 mL** | Antispasmodic |
| **Tincture of Valerian** | 1 in 5 | **2 – 4 mL** | Sedative |
| **Tincture of Colchicum** | 1 in 10 | **0.3 – 2 mL** | Gout |
| **Tincture of Lobelia (Ethereal)** | 1 in 8 | **0.6 – 2 mL** | Antiasthmatic |
| **Tincture of Cannabis** | 1 in 10 | Controlled | Sedative (obsolete) |
| ⭐ **Tincture of Iodine (Weak Iodine Solution)** | ⭐ **Iodine 2.5 % + KI 2.5 % in 90 % alcohol** | ⭐ **EXTERNAL ONLY** (antiseptic); ⚠️ internal dose obsolete 0.3 mL | Skin antiseptic |
| **Strong Iodine Solution (Lugol's)** | ⭐ **Iodine 5 % + KI 10 % in water** | **0.1 – 0.3 mL** well diluted | Thyrotoxicosis (pre-op) |
| **Tincture of Ferric Chloride (Strong)** | — | **0.3 – 1 mL** well diluted | Haematinic, astringent |
| **Tincture of Nutmeg** | 1 in 5 | 2 – 4 mL | Flavour, carminative |

🧠 **MEMORY HOOK — the tincture dose pattern:**
* **Potent (1 in 10) tinctures → small dose, ~0.3–2 mL.**
* **Flavouring/ bitter (1 in 5) tinctures → larger dose, ~2–4 mL.**
* **Very potent (aconite, capsicum) → 0.06–0.2 mL.**
* **Paregoric is the exception: weak, so 2–8 mL.**

### 📋 TABLE 7B — INFUSIONS, DECOCTIONS & LIQUID EXTRACTS

| Preparation | Strength | ⭐ Dose | Use |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Fresh Infusion of Senna** | 1 in 10 | **30 – 60 mL** | Purgative |
| **Fresh Infusion of Gentian (Compound)** | 1 in 10 | **15 – 30 mL** | Bitter tonic |
| **Fresh Infusion of Digitalis** | 1 in 10 | **2 – 8 mL** | Cardiotonic |
| **Fresh Infusion of Quassia** | 1 in 10 | **15 – 30 mL** | Bitter, enema for worms |
| **Fresh Infusion of Buchu / Orange peel** | 1 in 10 | 15 – 30 mL | Diuretic / flavour |
| ⭐ **Concentrated Infusion (any)** | ⭐ **8 × the fresh infusion** | ⭐ **Dose = ⅛ of the fresh infusion dose** (e.g. Conc. Inf. Gentian **2 – 4 mL**) | Dilute 1 + 7 with water |
| **Liquid Extract of Liquorice** | 1 in 1 | **2 – 4 mL** | Expectorant, flavour, demulcent |
| **Liquid Extract of Belladonna** | 1 in 1 | ⭐ **0.03 – 0.2 mL** | Antispasmodic |
| **Liquid Extract of Ipecacuanha** | 1 in 1 | **0.025 – 0.2 mL** | Expectorant |
| **Liquid Extract of Cascara** | 1 in 1 | **1 – 2 mL** | Laxative |
| **Liquid Extract of Senna** | 1 in 1 | **0.5 – 2 mL** | Purgative |
| **Liquid Extract of Ergot** | 1 in 1 | **0.5 – 2 mL** | Oxytocic |
| **Liquid Extract of Male Fern** | 1 in 1 | ⭐ **3 – 6 mL (max 8 mL)** ⚠️ toxic | Taenicide |
| **Liquid Extract of Nux Vomica** | 1 in 1 | 0.06 – 0.2 mL | Bitter tonic |
| **Belladonna Dry Extract** | — | **15 – 60 mg** | Antispasmodic |
| **Nux Vomica Dry Extract** | — | 15 – 60 mg | Tonic |

### 📋 TABLE 7C — SYRUPS, ELIXIRS & LINCTUSES

| Preparation | ⭐ Dose | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Simple Syrup (Syrup IP)** | Vehicle (66.7 % w/w sucrose) | Sweetening vehicle |
| **Syrup of Tolu** | **2 – 8 mL** | Expectorant flavour |
| **Syrup of Orange / Lemon / Raspberry / Blackcurrant** | 2 – 8 mL | Flavouring vehicle |
| **Syrup of Ginger** | 2 – 8 mL | Carminative flavour |
| **Syrup of Squill (Compound Syrup of Squill)** | **2 – 4 mL** | Expectorant |
| **Syrup of Ipecacuanha** | 0.5 – 2 mL | Expectorant |
| **Syrup of Senna** | **2 – 8 mL** | Laxative |
| **Syrup of Codeine Phosphate** | 5 mL (contains 15 mg) | Antitussive |
| **Syrup of Ferrous Sulphate / Ferrous Gluconate** | 5 – 10 mL | Haematinic |
| **Syrup of Chloral (Paediatric)** | 5 mL | Sedative |
| **Simple Linctus** | ⭐ **5 mL** (sipped slowly) | Demulcent cough |
| **Linctus of Codeine (Codeine Linctus)** | ⭐ **5 mL** (contains 15 mg codeine phosphate) | Cough suppressant |
| **Squill Opiate Linctus (Gee's Linctus)** | **5 mL** | Expectorant/ antitussive |
| **Elixir of Piperazine Citrate** | 5 mL (≈ 750 mg/5 mL) | Anthelmintic (roundworm) |
| **Paediatric Chloral Elixir** | 5 mL | Sedative for children |
| **Elixir of Phenobarbitone** | 5 mL | Sedative |
| **Aromatic Elixir** | Vehicle | Flavoured vehicle |

| Preparation | Strength | ⭐ Dose | Use |
|---|---|---|---|

| **Spirit of Peppermint** | 10 % | **0.3 – 2 mL** | Carminative, flavour |

| **Peppermint Water / Anise / Dill / Cinnamon / Fennel Water** | Saturated (~0.2 %) | **Vehicle; 15 mL as carminative** | Carminative vehicle |
| **Camphor Water** | Saturated | Vehicle | Eye lotion vehicle |
| **Rose Water** | Saturated | Vehicle | Perfumed vehicle (calamine lotion) |

|---|---|---|

| ⭐ **Compound Powder of Rhubarb (Gregory's Powder)** | Rhubarb 25 %, Heavy magnesium carbonate 32.5 %, Light magnesium oxide 32.5 %, Ginger 10 % | **1 – 4 g** — laxative & antacid |

| **Effervescent Powder of Magnesium Sulphate** | Mag. sulphate, sod. bicarb., citric acid, tartaric acid, sucrose | **5 – 10 g** in water — saline purgative |
| ⭐ **Oral Rehydration Salts (ORS — WHO/ IP)** | ⭐ **NaCl 2.6 g, Trisodium citrate dihydrate 2.9 g, KCl 1.5 g, Anhydrous glucose 13.5 g → dissolve in 1 litre of water** (reduced-osmolarity formula, 245 mOsm/L) | 1 sachet in 1 L; as needed after each loose stool |
| **Dusting Powder (Zinc, Starch & Talc)** | Zinc oxide 25 %, Starch 25 %, Talc 50 % | External |
| **Compound Powder of Zinc & Salicylic Acid (Lassar's)** | See Ch. 10 (paste) | External |
| **Insufflation** | See Ch. 11 | — |

🧠 **"Tragacanth powder = 15-20-20-45"** (Tragacanth, Acacia, Starch, Sucrose).
🧠 **"ORS = 2.6 – 2.9 – 1.5 – 13.5"** (NaCl, citrate, KCl, glucose).

Atropine 2 mg · Chloramphenicol & emetine — cumulative toxicity.**

---

## 7.5 ⭐ DOSAGE-FORM QUICK COMPARISONS (frequent discriminators)

🔁 **Mixture vs Draught** → **mixture = divided doses; draught = single 50 mL dose.**
🔁 **Syrup vs Elixir** → **syrup = aqueous, sugar-based, no/low alcohol; elixir = hydro-alcoholic,
clear, sweetened & aromatic.**
🔁 **Linctus vs Mixture** → **linctus is viscous, sweet, taken undiluted & sipped slowly for cough.**
🔁 **Tincture vs Liquid extract** → **tincture 1 in 5 or 1 in 10; liquid extract 1 in 1** (liquid
extract is therefore the more concentrated → smaller dose).
🔁 **Infusion vs Decoction** → **infusion = short maceration, no boiling; decoction = boiled.**
🔁 **Lotion vs Liniment** → **lotion applied WITHOUT friction; liniment applied WITH friction.**
🔁 **Emollient vs Demulcent** → **emollient softens SKIN (external); demulcent soothes MUCOUS
membrane (internal).**
🔁 **Gargle vs Mouthwash** → **gargle for the THROAT (usually diluted before use);
mouthwash for the MOUTH.**
🔁 **Collyrium vs Eye drops** → **collyrium = eye LOTION for bathing, preservative-free, discard in
24 h; eye drops = instilled, preserved, discard in 4 weeks.**
🔁 **Lozenge vs Pastille** → **lozenge = hard sugar-acacia base; pastille = soft glycero-gelatin
base.**
🔁 **Evacuant vs Retention enema** → **evacuant is expelled (cleanses bowel); retention is held for
absorption/ local action.**
🔁 **Bulk powder vs Divided powder** → **potent drugs MUST be dispensed as divided powders.**

---

## 7.6 CHAPTER 7 — RAPID REVISION SHEET

* **Posology = the study of dose.** Dose ladder: **minimum → therapeutic → maximum → toxic →
  lethal.**
* **Loading dose** = large initial dose to reach the effective level fast;
  **maintenance dose** keeps it there.
* 🔢 **Therapeutic Index = LD₅₀ ÷ ED₅₀; higher TI = safer.**
  **Narrow-TI drugs: digoxin, lithium, warfarin, phenytoin, theophylline, aminoglycosides.**
* **Factors affecting dose:** age, weight/BSA, sex (⚠️ pregnancy & lactation), route (⭐ parenteral
  dose < oral dose), dosage form, time relative to food, **tolerance, tachyphylaxis, cumulation,
  idiosyncrasy, allergy, habituation**, liver & kidney disease, **drug interaction (synergism/
  antagonism, enzyme induction/ inhibition)**, genetics, environment.
* **Cumulation** = elimination slower than administration (**digitalis, emetine, heavy metals,
  bromides**); **tolerance** = need for a larger dose on repetition (**morphine, barbiturates,
  nitrates**).
* **Enzyme inducers:** phenobarbitone, phenytoin, **rifampicin**, carbamazepine, griseofulvin,
  smoking. **Inhibitors:** cimetidine, erythromycin, ketoconazole, metronidazole, INH,
  chloramphenicol.
* 🔢 **STRENGTHS:** Tincture **1 in 5 (simple) / 1 in 10 (potent)** · Liquid extract **1 in 1** ·
  Fresh infusion **1 in 10, keeps 12 h** · Concentrated infusion **8×, dilute 1 + 7** ·
  Simple syrup **66.7 % w/w sucrose** · Mucilage of acacia **35 % w/v** ·
  Chloroform water **0.25 % v/v** (double strength 0.5 %) · Aromatic water **saturated (~0.2 %)** ·
  Spirit **5–10 %**.
* **Draught = single 50 mL dose. Linctus = 5 mL, viscous, sipped undiluted.**
* **Tincture of Iodine (weak) = Iodine 2.5 % + KI 2.5 % in 90 % alcohol; Lugol's (strong) =
  Iodine 5 % + KI 10 % in water.**
* **Compound Tincture of Benzoin = Friar's Balsam; inhalation 1 mL in 500 mL hot water.**
* **Paregoric = camphorated tincture of opium, dose 2–8 mL** (weak);
  **Laudanum = tincture of opium, dose 0.3–2 mL** (strong).
* **Dover's powder = Ipecac 10 % + Opium 10 % + Lactose 80 %; dose 300–600 mg.**
* **Compound tragacanth powder = 15 : 20 : 20 : 45.**
* **Gregory's powder = compound powder of rhubarb.**
* **ORS = NaCl 2.6 g + trisodium citrate 2.9 g + KCl 1.5 g + glucose 13.5 g per litre.**
* **Key doses:** Magnesium sulphate **5–15 g** · Castor oil **5–20 mL** · Liquid paraffin
  **10–30 mL** · Isapghula **4–16 g** · Ammonium chloride **0.3–2 g** · Ferrous sulphate
  **200 mg t.d.s.** · Paracetamol **500 mg–1 g (max 4 g/day)** · Atropine **0.6 mg** ·
  Adrenaline **0.5 mL of 1 : 1000 IM** · Male fern **3–6 mL (max 8)** · Activated charcoal
  **50–100 g** · Digoxin maintenance **0.125–0.25 mg**.
* **Eye drops: sterile, isotonic, preserved, discard 4 weeks after opening.
  Eye lotion (collyrium): preservative-free, discard 24 h.**
* **LVP must be isotonic, pyrogen-free and contain NO preservative.**
* **Potent drugs are dispensed as DIVIDED powders, never as a bulk powder.**

---
*Next → Chapter 8: Incompatibility — Physical, Chemical, Physiological & Therapeutical*


---

# CHAPTER 8
# ⚗️ INCOMPATIBILITY
### Physical · Chemical · Physiological (Therapeutical)

---

## 8.1 DEFINITION & IMPORTANCE

📌 **DEFINITION** — **Incompatibility** is an **undesirable change** that occurs when **two or more
substances (drugs, excipients or the container) are brought together**, resulting in a product that
is **physically unacceptable, chemically altered, therapeutically ineffective, or harmful to the
patient**.

📌 **In simple exam language:** *"Incompatibility is the phenomenon in which two or more ingredients
of a prescription react with each other, producing a change in the physical, chemical or therapeutic
properties of the preparation."*

⭐ **WHY IT MATTERS TO THE PHARMACIST**
* An incompatible mixture may be **inelegant** (unsightly precipitate), **inaccurate in dose**
  (the drug settles or decomposes), **therapeutically inactive**, or **actually toxic**.
* ⭐ Detecting an incompatibility **before compounding** is a **professional and legal duty** of the
  dispensing pharmacist.
* ⚠️ **The pharmacist must never dispense a knowingly incompatible mixture, and must never alter a
  prescription without the prescriber's consent.**

---

## 8.2 ⭐⭐ CLASSIFICATION OF INCOMPATIBILITY

```
                          INCOMPATIBILITY
                                 │
        ┌────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┐
        │                        │                        │
   ① PHYSICAL             ② CHEMICAL            ③ THERAPEUTIC
   (Pharmaceutical)                             (Physiological /
        │                        │               Pharmacological)
   No chemical            A chemical             No physical or
   reaction;              reaction occurs        chemical change;
   only physical          → new substance        the EFFECT in the
   change                 formed                 BODY is altered
        │                        │
   • Insolubility         Divided into:
   • Immiscibility        (a) IMMEDIATE (visible at once)
   • Liquefaction         (b) DELAYED  (develops on standing)
     (eutexia)            (c) TOLERATED / ADJUSTED
   • Precipitation            & UNADJUSTED / UNTOLERATED
     (physical)
```

⭐ **THE THREE TYPES — one-line discriminators (memorise!)**

| Type | What changes | Is a new compound formed? | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⭐ **PHYSICAL** | Only the **physical state/ appearance** | ❌ **No** | Camphor + menthol → liquefies |
| ⭐ **CHEMICAL** | **Chemical composition** — a **new substance** is formed | ✔ **Yes** | Tannic acid + alkaloid → precipitate |
| ⭐ **THERAPEUTIC** | The **pharmacological effect in the body** | ❌ No (in the bottle) | Morphine + strychnine (opposite actions) |

🧠 **MEMORY HOOK** — "**P**hysical = you can **see** it but nothing has **reacted**;
**C**hemical = something has **reacted**; **T**herapeutic = the **patient** is the one affected."

---

## 8.3 ① PHYSICAL (PHARMACEUTICAL) INCOMPATIBILITY ⭐⭐

📌 **DEFINITION** — Physical incompatibility occurs when the ingredients of a prescription **fail to
form a uniform, elegant, physically acceptable product**, **without any chemical reaction taking
place**. It is also called **pharmaceutical incompatibility**.

### ⭐ THE FIVE TYPES OF PHYSICAL INCOMPATIBILITY

### (A) INSOLUBILITY — *"a solid will not dissolve in the prescribed vehicle"*

| Situation | Example | ⭐ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| A resinous/ alcoholic tincture is added to an aqueous vehicle → **resin precipitates ("milkiness")** | ⭐ **Tincture of Benzoin, Tincture of Myrrh, Compound Tincture of Benzoin, Tincture of Tolu added to water** | Add a **suspending agent (tragacanth/ acacia mucilage)** and label **"Shake well"**; or add the tincture **slowly, with constant stirring, into the whole vehicle** (never the reverse) |
| A substance is insoluble in water | **Sulphur, calamine, zinc oxide, magnesium trisilicate, kaolin, chalk, aspirin, phenobarbitone** | Dispense as a **suspension** with a suspending agent + **"Shake well"** |
| Alkaloidal base is insoluble | Quinine base, morphine base | Use the **salt** (sulphate/ hydrochloride) instead — with the prescriber's consent |
| Volatile oil in water | Peppermint oil, clove oil | ⭐ Use an **aromatic water**, or triturate with **TALC/ light magnesium carbonate as a distributing agent** and filter, or add a **solubilising agent/ alcohol** |
| Substance soluble only in excess of another | Iodine (soluble in KI solution), calcium hydroxide | Add the **solubiliser** |

⭐ **The classical solubilisation tricks**
* **Alcohol/ glycerin/ propylene glycol** as a **cosolvent**.
* **Complexation:** ⭐ **Iodine dissolves in water in the presence of potassium iodide** (forms
  KI₃) — the basis of **Lugol's solution & Weak Iodine Solution**.
* **Caffeine dissolves in water in the presence of sodium benzoate/ citrate** (soluble complex).
* **Surfactant (Tween 80, polysorbate)** micellar solubilisation.
* **Salt formation** (converting a base into its hydrochloride/ sulphate).

### (B) IMMISCIBILITY — *"two liquids will not mix"*

| Example | ⭐ Correction |
|---|---|
| ⭐ **Fixed oil + water** (castor oil, cod liver oil, liquid paraffin with water) | ⭐ **Emulsify** — add an **emulsifying agent (acacia, tragacanth, gelatin, soap)** using the correct **oil : water : gum ratio** (fixed **4 : 2 : 1**, mineral **3 : 2 : 1**, volatile **2 : 2 : 1**, oleoresin **1 : 2 : 1**), and label **"Shake well"** |
| **Volatile oil (turpentine) + water** | Emulsify or use an aromatic water |
| **Oil + water in a liniment** | Nascent soap method (⭐ **olive oil + lime water = Carron oil/ Lime liniment**, emulgent = calcium oleate formed *in situ*) |
| **Chloroform/ ether + water** | Use as **chloroform water**, or emulsify |

### (C) LIQUEFACTION / DELIQUESCENCE ⭐ (the **EUTECTIC** problem)

📌 ⭐ **EUTECTIC MIXTURE** — a mixture of two or more solids which, when **triturated/ mixed
together**, **liquefies or softens** because the **melting point of the mixture is LOWER than the
melting point of either individual component**.

⭐⭐ **THE CLASSIC EUTECTIC SUBSTANCES — memorise this list!**
**Camphor · Menthol · Thymol · Phenol · Chloral hydrate · Betanaphthol · Resorcinol · Salol
(phenyl salicylate) · Antipyrine · Phenacetin · Acetanilide · Aspirin · Urethane · Pyrocatechol ·
Chloral + camphor ("chloral camphor")**

🧠 **MEMORY HOOK — "CAMP-MEN-THY-PHE-CHLO"** →
**Camp**hor, **Men**thol, **Thy**mol, **Phe**nol, **Chlo**ral hydrate — the five that turn up in
almost every question.

⭐ **CORRECTION of a eutectic (liquefaction) incompatibility**

| Method | How |
|---|---|
| ⭐ **1. Mix with the LEAST possible pressure, on a TILE with a SPATULA (spatulation)** | Never triturate hard in a mortar — pressure & friction cause liquefaction |
| ⭐ **2. Add an ABSORBENT / inert diluent (the commonest method)** | Add **light magnesium carbonate, light kaolin, magnesium oxide, starch, lactose, talc or bentonite** — each drug is **triturated separately with a portion of the absorbent** and the two are then **mixed gently** |
| ⭐ **3. Allow the liquefaction to occur deliberately, then absorb it** | Let the mixture form a liquid, then **incorporate an absorbent** to make a dry powder |
| **4. Dispense the components SEPARATELY** | In separate powder papers, to be mixed by the patient |
| ⭐ **5. Dispense in a CACHET or CAPSULE, or use double-wrapped WAXED powder papers** | Prevents moisture & leakage |
| **6. Convert into a liquid/ semisolid form** | Dissolve in a suitable solvent, or dispense as an ointment/ paste |
| **7. Use the eutectic deliberately** | ⭐ Sometimes desirable: **EMLA cream = eutectic mixture of lidocaine + prilocaine** (a therapeutic use!); a eutectic also helps in **fusing suppository bases** |

⚠️ **TRAP** — *Liquefaction of a eutectic is a **PHYSICAL** incompatibility, not chemical* —
no new compound is formed; the drugs are simply **mutually soluble in each other's melt**.

⭐ **DELIQUESCENT & HYGROSCOPIC substances also cause "physical" dampness in powders:**
**Calcium chloride, zinc chloride, potassium/ sodium hydroxide, ferric chloride, ammonium nitrate,
pepsin, trypsin, sodium iodide, potassium citrate, ammonium chloride, glycerin, sorbitol.**
⭐ **Correction:** add an absorbent (light magnesium carbonate/ starch), dispense in
**air-tight containers** with a **desiccant**, or use **double-waxed powder papers**.

⭐ **EFFLORESCENT substances** (they *give out* water of crystallisation and make a powder damp):
**Sodium carbonate (Na₂CO₃·10H₂O), ferrous sulphate, magnesium sulphate, sodium sulphate,
alum, atropine sulphate, codeine phosphate, citric acid, quinine bisulphate, caffeine.**
⭐ **Correction:** ⭐ use the **exsiccated (anhydrous/ dried) form** — e.g. **exsiccated ferrous
